class="infobox-label"><a href="/wiki/Franco_Coppola" title="Franco Coppola">Franco Coppola</a></th><td class="infobox-data">12 September 2009</td></tr><tr><th scope="row" class="infobox-label"><a 2013</td></tr></tbody></table></td></tr><tr style="display:none"><td colspan="2">
  </td></tr><tr><td colspan="2" class="infobox-below"><a href="/wiki/Pope_Benedict_(disambiguation)" class="mw-redirect mw-disambig" title="Pope Benedict (disambiguation)">Other popes  named Benedict</a></td></tr></tbody></table>
<link rel="mw-deduplicated-inline-style" href="mw-data:TemplateStyles:r1066479718"/><table class="infobox vcard"><tbody><tr><th colspan="2" class="infobox-above" style="font-weight: normal; background-color: gold;"><span style="font-size:80%">Papal styles of</span><br /><b><span class="fn">Pope Benedict XVI</span></b></th></tr><tr><td colspan="2" class="infobox-image"><a href="/wiki/File:Coat_of_Arms_of_Benedictus_XVI.svg" class="image"><img alt="Coat of Arms of Benedictus XVI.svg" src="//upload.wikimedia.org/wikipedia/commons/thumb/6/68/Coat_of_Arms_of_Benedictus_XVI.svg/200px-Coat_of_Arms_of_Benedictus_XVI.svg.png" decoding="async" width="200" height="268" srcset="//upload.wikimedia.org/wikipedia/commons/thumb/6/68/Coat_of_Arms_of_Benedictus_XVI.svg/300px-Coat_of_Arms_of_Benedictus_XVI.svg.png 1.5x, //upload.wikimedia.org/wikipedia/commons/thumb/6/68/Coat_of_Arms_of_Benedictus_XVI.svg/400px-Coat_of_Arms_of_Benedictus_XVI.svg.png 2x" data-file-width="762" data-file-height="1021" /></a></td></tr><tr><th scope="row" class="infobox-label"><a href="/wiki/Style_(manner_of_address)" class="mw-redirect" title="Style (manner of address)">Reference style</a></th><td class="infobox-data"><a href="/wiki/His_Holiness" class="mw-redirect" title="His Holiness">His Holiness</a></td></tr><tr><th scope="row" class="infobox-label">Spoken style</th><td class="infobox-data">Your Holiness</td></tr><tr><th scope="row" class="infobox-label">Religious style</th><td class="infobox-data">Holy Father</td></tr></tbody></table>
<p><b>Pope Benedict XVI</b> (<a href="/wiki/Latin_language" class="mw-redirect" title="Latin language">Latin</a>: <i lang="la">Benedictus XVI</i>; <a href="/wiki/Italian_language" title="Italian language">Italian</a>: <i lang="it">Benedetto XVI</i>; <a href="/wiki/German_language" title="German language">German</a>: <i lang="de">Benedikt XVI</i>; born <b>Joseph Aloisius Ratzinger</b>; 16 April 1927 – 31 December 2022) was the <a href="/wiki/Head_of_the_Catholic_Church" class="mw-redirect" title="Head of the Catholic Church">head of the Catholic Church</a> and sovereign of the <a href="/wiki/Vatican_City_State" class="mw-redirect" title="Vatican City State">Vatican City State</a> from 19 April 2005 until <a href="/wiki/Resignation_of_Pope_Benedict_XVI" title="Resignation of Pope Benedict XVI">his resignation</a> on 28 February 2013. Benedict's election as pope occurred in the <a href="/wiki/2005_papal_conclave" title="2005 papal conclave">2005 papal conclave</a> that followed the <a href="/wiki/Death_of_Pope_John_Paul_II" class="mw-redirect" title="Death of Pope John Paul II">death of Pope John Paul&#160;II</a>. Benedict chose to be known as "<a href="/wiki/Pope_emeritus" class="mw-redirect" title="Pope emeritus">Pope emeritus</a>" upon his resignation, and he retained this title until <a href="/wiki/Death_and_funeral_of_Pope_Benedict_XVI" title="Death and funeral of Pope Benedict XVI">his death</a> in December 2022.<sup id="cite_ref-pope_emeritus_10-0" class="reference"><a href="#cite_note-pope_emeritus-10">&#91;10&#93;</a></sup><sup id="cite_ref-pope_emeritus_ncr_11-0" class="reference"><a href="#cite_note-pope_emeritus_ncr-11">&#91;11&#93;</a></sup>
</p><p>Ordained as a <a href="/wiki/Priesthood_in_the_Catholic_Church" title="Priesthood in the Catholic Church">priest</a> in 1951 in his native <a href="/wiki/Bavaria" title="Bavaria">Bavaria</a>, Ratzinger embarked on an <a href="/wiki/Academy" title="Academy">academic</a> career and established himself as a highly regarded theologian by the late 1950s. He was appointed a full professor in 1958 at the age of 31. After a long career as a professor of theology at several German universities, he was appointed <a href="/wiki/Archbishop_of_Munich_and_Freising" class="mw-redirect" title="Archbishop of Munich and Freising">Archbishop of Munich and Freising</a> and created a <a href="/wiki/Cardinal_(Catholic_Church)" title="Cardinal (Catholic Church)">cardinal</a> by <a href="/wiki/Pope_Paul_VI" title="Pope Paul VI">Pope Paul VI</a> in 1977, an unusual promotion for someone with little pastoral experience. In 1981, he was appointed Prefect of the <a href="/wiki/Congregation_for_the_Doctrine_of_the_Faith" class="mw-redirect" title="Congregation for the Doctrine of the Faith">Congregation for the Doctrine of the Faith</a>, one of the most important <a href="/wiki/Dicasteries" class="mw-redirect" title="Dicasteries">dicasteries</a> of the <a href="/wiki/Roman_Curia" title="Roman Curia">Roman Curia</a>. From 2002 until he was elected pope, he was also <a href="/wiki/Dean_of_the_College_of_Cardinals" title="Dean of the College of Cardinals">Dean of the College of Cardinals</a>. Before becoming pope, he was "a major figure on the <a href="/wiki/Holy_See" title="Holy See">Vatican</a> stage for a quarter of a century"; he had an influence "second to none when it came to setting church priorities and directions" as one of <a href="/wiki/John_Paul_II" class="mw-redirect" title="John Paul II">John Paul&#160;II</a>'s closest confidants.<sup id="cite_ref-12" class="reference"><a href="#cite_note-12">&#91;12&#93;</a></sup>
</p><p>Benedict's writings were prolific and generally defended traditional Catholic doctrine, values, and liturgy.<sup id="cite_ref-13" class="reference"><a href="#cite_note-13">&#91;13&#93;</a></sup> He was originally a <a href="/wiki/Liberal_Catholicism" title="Liberal Catholicism">liberal theologian</a> but adopted conservative views after 1968.<sup id="cite_ref-14" class="reference"><a href="#cite_note-14">&#91;14&#93;</a></sup> During his papacy, Benedict advocated a return to fundamental <a href="/wiki/Christian_values" title="Christian values">Christian values</a> to counter the increased <a href="/wiki/Secularisation" class="mw-redirect" title="Secularisation">secularisation</a> of many <a href="/wiki/Western_countries" class="mw-redirect" title="Western countries">Western countries</a>. He viewed <a href="/wiki/Relativism" title="Relativism">relativism</a>'s denial of <a href="/wiki/Objectivity_(philosophy)" title="Objectivity (philosophy)">objective truth</a>, and the denial of <a href="/wiki/Moral_relativism" title="Moral relativism">moral truths</a> in particular, as the central problem of the 21st century. He taught the importance of both the <a href="/wiki/Catholic_Church" title="Catholic Church">Catholic Church</a> and an understanding of God's redemptive love.<sup id="cite_ref-15" class="reference"><a href="#cite_note-15">&#91;15&#93;</a></sup> Benedict also revived several traditions, including the <a href="/wiki/Tridentine_Mass" title="Tridentine Mass">Tridentine Mass</a>.<sup id="cite_ref-16" class="reference"><a href="#cite_note-16">&#91;16&#93;</a></sup> He strengthened the relationship between the <a href="/wiki/Catholic_Church_and_art" class="mw-redirect" title="Catholic Church and art">Catholic Church and art</a>, promoted the use of <a href="/wiki/Latin" title="Latin">Latin</a>,<sup id="cite_ref-17" class="reference"><a href="#cite_note-17">&#91;17&#93;</a></sup> and reintroduced traditional <a href="/wiki/Papal_vestments" class="mw-redirect" title="Papal vestments">papal vestments</a>, for which reason he was called "the pope of aesthetics".<sup id="cite_ref-latimes1_18-0" class="reference"><a href="#cite_note-latimes1-18">&#91;18&#93;</a></sup> He was described as "the main intellectual force in the Church" since the mid-1980s.<sup id="cite_ref-19" class="reference"><a href="#cite_note-19">&#91;19&#93;</a></sup>
</p><p>On 11 February 2013, Benedict announced his resignation, citing a "lack of strength of mind and body" due to his advanced age. His resignation was the first by a pope since <a href="/wiki/Gregory_XII" class="mw-redirect" title="Gregory XII">Gregory XII</a> in 1415, and the first on a pope's initiative since <a href="/wiki/Celestine_V" class="mw-redirect" title="Celestine V">Celestine V</a> in 1294. He was succeeded by <a href="/wiki/Pope_Francis" title="Pope Francis">Francis</a> on 13 March 2013 and moved into the newly renovated <a href="/wiki/Mater_Ecclesiae_Monastery" title="Mater Ecclesiae Monastery">Mater Ecclesiae Monastery</a> in Vatican City for his retirement. In addition to his native German language, Benedict had some level of proficiency in French, Italian, English, and Spanish. He also knew Portuguese, Latin, <a href="/wiki/Biblical_Hebrew" title="Biblical Hebrew">Biblical Hebrew</a>, and <a href="/wiki/Biblical_Greek" class="mw-redirect" title="Biblical Greek">Biblical Greek</a>.<sup id="cite_ref-20" class="reference"><a href="#cite_note-20">&#91;20&#93;</a></sup><sup id="cite_ref-21" class="reference"><a href="#cite_note-21">&#91;21&#93;</a></sup><sup id="cite_ref-22" class="reference"><a href="#cite_note-22">&#91;22&#93;</a></sup> He was a member of several <a href="/wiki/Social_science" title="Social science">social science</a> academies, such as the French <a href="/wiki/Acad%C3%A9mie_des_Sciences_Morales_et_Politiques" title="Académie des Sciences Morales et Politiques">Académie des Sciences Morales et Politiques</a>. He played the piano and had a preference for <a href="/wiki/Mozart" class="mw-redirect" title="Mozart">Mozart</a> and <a href="/wiki/Bach" class="mw-redirect" title="Bach">Bach</a>.<sup id="cite_ref-Willey_23-0" class="reference"><a href="#cite_note-Willey-23">&#91;23&#93;</a></sup>
</p><p>Benedict's handling of <a href="/wiki/Sexual_abuse_cases_within_the_Catholic_Church" class="mw-redirect" title="Sexual abuse cases within the Catholic Church">sexual abuse cases within the Catholic Church</a> and opposition to usage of <a href="/wiki/Condoms" class="mw-redirect" title="Condoms">condoms</a> in areas of <a href="/wiki/HIV/AIDS_in_Africa" title="HIV/AIDS in Africa">high HIV transmission</a> led to substantial criticism from public health officials, anti-AIDS activists, and victim's rights organizations.<sup id="cite_ref-24" class="reference"><a href="#cite_note-24">&#91;24&#93;</a></sup><sup id="cite_ref-25" class="reference"><a href="#cite_note-25">&#91;25&#93;</a></sup>
</p>
<meta property="mw:PageProp/toc" />
<h2><span id="Early_life:_1927.E2.80.931951"></span><span class="mw-headline" id="Early_life:_1927–1951">Early life: 1927–1951</span></h2>
<style data-mw-deduplicate="TemplateStyles:r1033289096">.mw-parser-output .hatnote{font-style:italic}.mw-parser-output div.hatnote{padding-left:1.6em;margin-bottom:0.5em}.mw-parser-output .hatnote i{font-style:normal}.mw-parser-output .hatnote+link+.hatnote{margin-top:-0.5em}</style><div role="note" class="hatnote navigation-not-searchable">Main article: <a href="/wiki/Early_life_of_Pope_Benedict_XVI" title="Early life of Pope Benedict XVI">Early life of Pope Benedict XVI</a></div>
<div class="thumb tright"><div class="thumbinner" style="width:242px;"><a href="/wiki/File:PopeBenedicts1stHome.jpg" class="image"><img alt="" src="//upload.wikimedia.org/wikipedia/commons/thumb/0/07/PopeBenedicts1stHome.jpg/240px-PopeBenedicts1stHome.jpg" decoding="async" width="240" height="180" class="thumbimage" srcset="//upload.wikimedia.org/wikipedia/commons/thumb/0/07/PopeBenedicts1stHome.jpg/360px-PopeBenedicts1stHome.jpg 1.5x, //upload.wikimedia.org/wikipedia/commons/thumb/0/07/PopeBenedicts1stHome.jpg/480px-PopeBenedicts1stHome.jpg 2x" data-file-width="1417" data-file-height="1063" /></a>  <div class="thumbcaption"><div class="magnify"><a href="/wiki/File:PopeBenedicts1stHome.jpg" class="internal" title="Enlarge"></a></div>The birth house of Joseph Aloisius Ratzinger in <a href="/wiki/Marktl" title="Marktl">Marktl</a>, Bavaria</div></div></div>
<p>Joseph Aloisius Ratzinger (<small>German:&#32;</small><span title="Representation in the International Phonetic Alphabet (IPA)" class="IPA" lang="de-Latn-fonipa"><a href="/wiki/Help:IPA/Standard_German" title="Help:IPA/Standard German">[ˈjoːzɛf ʔaˈlɔʏzi̯ʊs ˈʁatsɪŋɐ]</a></span>), later Pope Benedict XVI (<a href="/wiki/Italian_language" title="Italian language">Italian</a>: <i lang="it">Benedetto XVI</i>; German: <i lang="de">Benedikt XVI</i>) was born on 16 April, <a href="/wiki/Holy_Saturday" title="Holy Saturday">Holy Saturday</a>, 1927, at Schulstraße 11, at 8:30 in the morning in his parents' home in Marktl, Bavaria, Germany. He was baptised the same day. He was the third and youngest child of Joseph Ratzinger Sr., a police officer, and Maria Ratzinger (<span class="avoidwrap" style="display:inline-block;" title="Name at birth"><a href="/wiki/N%C3%A9e" class="mw-redirect" title="Née">née</a> Peintner</span>); his grand-uncle was the German priest-politician <a href="/wiki/Georg_Ratzinger_(politician)" title="Georg Ratzinger (politician)">Georg Ratzinger</a>. His mother's family was originally from <a href="/wiki/South_Tyrol" title="South Tyrol">South Tyrol</a> (now in Italy).<sup id="cite_ref-26" class="reference"><a href="#cite_note-26">&#91;26&#93;</a></sup> Benedict's elder brother, <a href="/wiki/Georg_Ratzinger" title="Georg Ratzinger">Georg Ratzinger</a>, became a Catholic priest and was the former director of the <a href="/wiki/Regensburger_Domspatzen" title="Regensburger Domspatzen">Regensburger Domspatzen</a> choir.<sup id="cite_ref-27" class="reference"><a href="#cite_note-27">&#91;27&#93;</a></sup> His sister, Maria Ratzinger, who never married, managed her brother Joseph's household until she died in 1991.<sup id="cite_ref-28" class="reference"><a href="#cite_note-28">&#91;28&#93;</a></sup>
</p><p>At the age of five, Ratzinger was in a group of children who welcomed the visiting Cardinal Archbishop of Munich, <a href="/wiki/Michael_von_Faulhaber" title="Michael von Faulhaber">Michael von Faulhaber</a>, with flowers. Struck by the cardinal's distinctive garb, he announced later that day that he wanted to be a cardinal. He attended the elementary school in <a href="/wiki/Aschau_am_Inn" title="Aschau am Inn">Aschau am Inn</a>, which was renamed in his honor in 2009.<sup id="cite_ref-29" class="reference"><a href="#cite_note-29">&#91;29&#93;</a></sup> In 1939, aged 12, he enrolled in a <a href="/wiki/Minor_seminary" title="Minor seminary">minor seminary</a> in <a href="/wiki/Traunstein" title="Traunstein">Traunstein</a>.<sup id="cite_ref-30" class="reference"><a href="#cite_note-30">&#91;30&#93;</a></sup> This period lasted until the seminary was closed for military use in 1942, and the students were all sent home. Ratzinger returned to Traunstein.<sup id="cite_ref-31" class="reference"><a href="#cite_note-31">&#91;31&#93;</a></sup>
</p>
<h3><span class="mw-headline" id="Wartime_and_ordination">Wartime and ordination</span></h3>
<p>Ratzinger's family, especially his father, bitterly resented the <a href="/wiki/Nazis" class="mw-redirect" title="Nazis">Nazis</a>, and his father's opposition to Nazism resulted in demotions and harassment of the family.<sup id="cite_ref-32" class="reference"><a href="#cite_note-32">&#91;32&#93;</a></sup> Following his 14th birthday in 1941, Ratzinger was conscripted into the <a href="/wiki/Hitler_Youth" title="Hitler Youth">Hitler Youth</a>—as membership was required by law for all 14-year-old German boys after March 1939<sup id="cite_ref-33" class="reference"><a href="#cite_note-33">&#91;33&#93;</a></sup>—but was an unenthusiastic member who refused to attend meetings, according to his brother.<sup id="cite_ref-USAToday_20050423_34-0" class="reference"><a href="#cite_note-USAToday_20050423-34">&#91;34&#93;</a></sup> In 1941, one of Ratzinger's cousins, a 14-year-old boy with <a href="/wiki/Down_syndrome" title="Down syndrome">Down syndrome</a>, was taken away by the Nazi regime and murdered during the <a href="/wiki/Action_T4" class="mw-redirect" title="Action T4">Action T4</a> campaign of <a href="/wiki/Nazi_eugenics" title="Nazi eugenics">Nazi eugenics</a>.<sup id="cite_ref-35" class="reference"><a href="#cite_note-35">&#91;35&#93;</a></sup> In 1943, while still in seminary, he was drafted into the German anti-aircraft corps as <a href="/wiki/Luftwaffenhelfer" title="Luftwaffenhelfer">Luftwaffenhelfer</a>.<sup id="cite_ref-USAToday_20050423_34-1" class="reference"><a href="#cite_note-USAToday_20050423-34">&#91;34&#93;</a></sup> Ratzinger then trained in the German infantry.<sup id="cite_ref-36" class="reference"><a href="#cite_note-36">&#91;36&#93;</a></sup> As the Allied front drew closer to his post in 1945, he deserted back to his family's home in <a href="/wiki/Traunstein" title="Traunstein">Traunstein</a> after his unit had ceased to exist, just as American troops established a headquarters in the Ratzinger household.<sup id="cite_ref-FoxNews_19April2005_37-0" class="reference"><a href="#cite_note-FoxNews_19April2005-37">&#91;37&#93;</a></sup> As a German soldier, he was <a href="/wiki/Internment" title="Internment">interned</a> in US <a href="/wiki/Prisoner_of_war" title="Prisoner of war">prisoner of war</a> camps, first in Neu-Ulm, then at Fliegerhorst Bad Aibling (shortly to be repurposed as <a href="/wiki/Bad_Aibling_Station" title="Bad Aibling Station">Bad Aibling Station</a>) where he was at the time of <a href="/wiki/Victory_in_Europe_Day" title="Victory in Europe Day">Victory in Europe Day</a>, and released on 19 June 1945.<sup id="cite_ref-38" class="reference"><a href="#cite_note-38">&#91;38&#93;</a></sup><sup id="cite_ref-FoxNews_19April2005_37-1" class="reference"><a href="#cite_note-FoxNews_19April2005-37">&#91;37&#93;</a></sup>
</p><p>Ratzinger and his brother Georg entered Saint Michael Seminary in Traunstein in November 1945, later studying at the <a href="/wiki/Ducal_Georgianum" title="Ducal Georgianum">Ducal Georgianum</a> (<span title="German-language text"><i lang="de">Herzogliches Georgianum</i></span>) of the <a href="/wiki/Ludwig_Maximilian_University_of_Munich" title="Ludwig Maximilian University of Munich">Ludwig Maximilian University</a> in Munich. They were both ordained in <a href="/wiki/Freising" title="Freising">Freising</a> on 29 June 1951 by Cardinal <a href="/wiki/Michael_von_Faulhaber" title="Michael von Faulhaber">Michael von Faulhaber</a> of Munich&#160;&#8211;&#160;the same man Ratzinger had met as a child. He recalled: "at the moment the elderly Archbishop laid his hands on me, a little bird&#160;&#8211;&#160;perhaps a lark&#160;&#8211;&#160;flew up from the altar in the high cathedral and trilled a little joyful song".<sup id="cite_ref-39" class="reference"><a href="#cite_note-39">&#91;39&#93;</a></sup>
</p><p>Ratzinger's 1953 dissertation was on <a href="/wiki/Augustine_of_Hippo" title="Augustine of Hippo">Augustine of Hippo</a> and was titled <i>The People and the House of God in Augustine's Doctrine of the Church</i>. His <a href="/wiki/Habilitation" title="Habilitation">habilitation</a> (which qualified him for a professorship) was on <a href="/wiki/Bonaventure" title="Bonaventure">Bonaventure</a>. It was completed in 1957 and he became a professor at Freising College in 1958.<sup id="cite_ref-40" class="reference"><a href="#cite_note-40">&#91;40&#93;</a></sup>
</p>
<h3><span class="mw-headline" id="Encounter_with_Romano_Guardini">Encounter with Romano Guardini</span></h3>
<p>In his early twenties, Ratzinger was deeply influenced by the thought of Italian German <a href="/wiki/Romano_Guardini" title="Romano Guardini">Romano Guardini</a>,<sup id="cite_ref-41" class="reference"><a href="#cite_note-41">&#91;41&#93;</a></sup> who taught in Munich from 1946 to 1951 when Ratzinger was studying in Freising and later at the University of Munich. The intellectual affinity between these two thinkers, who would later become decisive figures for the twentieth-century Catholic Church, was preoccupied with rediscovering the essentials of Christianity: Guardini wrote his 1938 <i>The Essence of Christianity</i>, while Ratzinger penned <i>Introduction to Christianity</i>, three decades later in 1968. Guardini inspired many in the Catholic social-democratic tradition, particularly the <a href="/wiki/Communion_and_Liberation" title="Communion and Liberation">Communion and Liberation</a> movement in the <a href="/wiki/New_Evangelization" class="mw-redirect" title="New Evangelization">New Evangelization</a> encouraged under the papacy of the Polish Pope <a href="/wiki/John_Paul_II" class="mw-redirect" title="John Paul II">John Paul II</a>. Ratzinger wrote an introduction to a 1996 reissue of Guardini's 1954 <i><a href="/wiki/The_Lord_(book)" title="The Lord (book)">The Lord</a></i>.<sup id="cite_ref-42" class="reference"><a href="#cite_note-42">&#91;42&#93;</a></sup>
</p>
<h2><span id="Pre-papal_career:_1951.E2.80.932005"></span><span class="mw-headline" id="Pre-papal_career:_1951–2005">Pre-papal career: 1951–2005</span></h2>

</tr><tr><td class="sidebar-below">
<div class="hlist"><ul><li><a href="/wiki/File:046CupolaSPietro.jpg" class="image"><img alt="icon" src="//upload.wikimedia.org/wikipedia/commons/thumb/5/5a/046CupolaSPietro.jpg/16px-046CupolaSPietro.jpg" decoding="async" width="16" height="12" class="noviewer thumbborder" srcset="//upload.wikimedia.org/wikipedia/commons/thumb/5/5a/046CupolaSPietro.jpg/24px-046CupolaSPietro.jpg 1.5x, //upload.wikimedia.org/wikipedia/commons/thumb/5/5a/046CupolaSPietro.jpg/32px-046CupolaSPietro.jpg 2x" data-file-width="800" data-file-height="600" /></a>&#160;<a href="/wiki/Portal:Catholicism" class="mw-redirect" title="Portal:Catholicism">Catholicism&#32;portal</a></li><li><img alt="" src="//upload.wikimedia.org/wikipedia/commons/thumb/c/cd/Socrates.png/10px-Socrates.png" decoding="async" width="10" height="16" class="noviewer" srcset="//upload.wikimedia.org/wikipedia/commons/thumb/c/cd/Socrates.png/15px-Socrates.png 1.5x, //upload.wikimedia.org/wikipedia/commons/thumb/c/cd/Socrates.png/21px-Socrates.png 2x" data-file-width="326" data-file-height="500" />&#160;<a href="/wiki/Portal:Philosophy" title="Portal:Philosophy">Philosophy&#32;portal</a></li></ul></div></td></tr><tr><td class="sidebar-navbar"><link rel="mw-deduplicated-inline-style" href="mw-data:TemplateStyles:r1129693374"/><link rel="mw-deduplicated-inline-style" href="mw-data:TemplateStyles:r1063604349"/><div class="navbar plainlinks hlist navbar-mini"><ul><li class="nv-view"><a href="/wiki/Template:Catholic_philosophy" title="Template:Catholic philosophy"><abbr title="View this template">v</abbr></a></li><li class="nv-talk"><a href="/wiki/Template_talk:Catholic_philosophy" title="Template talk:Catholic philosophy"><abbr title="Discuss this template">t</abbr></a></li><li class="nv-edit"><a class="external text" href="https://en.wikipedia.org/w/index.php?title=Template:Catholic_philosophy&amp;action=edit"><abbr title="Edit this template">e</abbr></a></li></ul></div></td></tr></tbody></table>
<p>Ratzinger began as assistant pastor (curate) at the parish <a href="/wiki/St._Martin,_Moosach" title="St. Martin, Moosach">St. Martin, Moosach</a>, in Munich in 1951.<sup id="cite_ref-43" class="reference"><a href="#cite_note-43">&#91;43&#93;</a></sup> Ratzinger became a professor at the <a href="/wiki/University_of_Bonn" title="University of Bonn">University of Bonn</a> in 1959, with his inaugural lecture on "The God of Faith and the God of Philosophy". In 1963, he moved to the <a href="/wiki/University_of_M%C3%BCnster" title="University of Münster">University of Münster</a>. During this period, he participated in the <a href="/wiki/Second_Vatican_Council" title="Second Vatican Council">Second Vatican Council</a> (1962–1965) and served as a <i><a href="/wiki/Peritus" title="Peritus">peritus</a></i> (theological consultant) to <a href="/wiki/Josef_Frings" title="Josef Frings">Cardinal Frings of Cologne</a>. He was viewed during the time of the council as a reformer, cooperating with theologians like <a href="/wiki/Hans_K%C3%BCng" title="Hans Küng">Hans Küng</a><sup id="cite_ref-44" class="reference"><a href="#cite_note-44">&#91;44&#93;</a></sup> and <a href="/wiki/Edward_Schillebeeckx" title="Edward Schillebeeckx">Edward Schillebeeckx</a>.<sup id="cite_ref-45" class="reference"><a href="#cite_note-45">&#91;45&#93;</a></sup> Ratzinger became an admirer of <a href="/wiki/Karl_Rahner" title="Karl Rahner">Karl Rahner</a>, a well-known academic theologian of the <a href="/wiki/Nouvelle_Th%C3%A9ologie" class="mw-redirect" title="Nouvelle Théologie">Nouvelle Théologie</a> and a proponent of Church reform.<sup id="cite_ref-46" class="reference"><a href="#cite_note-46">&#91;46&#93;</a></sup>
</p><p>In 1966, Ratzinger was appointed to a chair in dogmatic theology at the <a href="/wiki/University_of_T%C3%BCbingen" title="University of Tübingen">University of Tübingen</a>, where he was a colleague of <a href="/wiki/Hans_K%C3%BCng" title="Hans Küng">Hans Küng</a>. In his 1968 book <i><a href="/wiki/Introduction_to_Christianity" title="Introduction to Christianity">Introduction to Christianity</a></i>, he wrote that the pope has a duty to hear differing voices within the Church before making a decision, and he downplayed the centrality of the papacy. During this time, he distanced himself from the atmosphere of Tübingen and the <a href="/wiki/Marxism" title="Marxism">Marxist</a> leanings of the student movement of the 1960s that quickly radicalised, in the years 1967 and 1968, culminating in a series of disturbances and riots in April and May 1968. Ratzinger came increasingly to see these and associated developments (such as decreasing respect for authority among his students) as connected to a departure from traditional Catholic teachings.<sup id="cite_ref-sobene_47-0" class="reference"><a href="#cite_note-sobene-47">&#91;47&#93;</a></sup> Despite his reformist bent, his views increasingly came to contrast with the liberal ideas gaining currency in theological circles.<sup id="cite_ref-48" class="reference"><a href="#cite_note-48">&#91;48&#93;</a></sup> He was invited by Rev. <a href="/wiki/Theodore_Hesburgh" title="Theodore Hesburgh">Theodore Hesburgh</a> to join the theology faculty at the <a href="/wiki/University_of_Notre_Dame" title="University of Notre Dame">University of Notre Dame</a>, but declined on grounds that his English was not good enough.<sup id="cite_ref-49" class="reference"><a href="#cite_note-49">&#91;49&#93;</a></sup>
</p><p>Some voices, among them Küng, deemed this period in Ratzinger's life a turn towards conservatism, while Ratzinger himself said in a 1993 interview, "I see no break in my views as a theologian [over the years]".<sup id="cite_ref-50" class="reference"><a href="#cite_note-50">&#91;50&#93;</a></sup> Ratzinger continued to defend the work of the Second Vatican Council, including <i><a href="/wiki/Nostra_aetate" title="Nostra aetate">Nostra aetate</a></i>, the document on respect of other religions, <a href="/wiki/Ecumenism" title="Ecumenism">ecumenism</a>, and the declaration of the right to <a href="/wiki/Freedom_of_religion" title="Freedom of religion">freedom of religion</a>. Later, as the <a href="/wiki/Joseph_Ratzinger_as_Prefect_of_the_Congregation_for_the_Doctrine_of_the_Faith" title="Joseph Ratzinger as Prefect of the Congregation for the Doctrine of the Faith">Prefect of the Congregation for the Doctrine of the Faith</a>, Ratzinger most clearly spelled out the Catholic Church's position on other religions in the 2000 document <i><a href="/wiki/Dominus_Iesus" title="Dominus Iesus">Dominus Iesus</a></i> which also talks about the Catholic way to engage in "<a href="/wiki/Catholic_Church_and_ecumenism" title="Catholic Church and ecumenism">ecumenical</a> dialogue". During his time at Tübingen University, Ratzinger published articles in the reformist theological journal <i><a href="/wiki/Concilium_(journal)" title="Concilium (journal)">Concilium</a></i>, though he increasingly chose less reformist themes than other contributors to the magazine such as Küng and Schillebeeckx.<sup id="cite_ref-51" class="reference"><a href="#cite_note-51">&#91;51&#93;</a></sup>
</p><p>In 1969, Ratzinger returned to Bavaria, to the <a href="/wiki/University_of_Regensburg" title="University of Regensburg">University of Regensburg</a> and co-founded the theological journal <i><a href="/wiki/Communio" title="Communio">Communio</a></i>, with <a href="/wiki/Hans_Urs_von_Balthasar" title="Hans Urs von Balthasar">Hans Urs von Balthasar</a>, <a href="/wiki/Henri_de_Lubac" title="Henri de Lubac">Henri de Lubac</a>, <a href="/wiki/Walter_Kasper" title="Walter Kasper">Walter Kasper</a>, and others, in 1972. <i>Communio</i>, now published in seventeen languages, including German, English, and Spanish, has become a prominent journal of contemporary Catholic theological thought. Until he was elected pope, he remained one of the journal's most prolific contributors. In 1976, he suggested that the <a href="/wiki/Augsburg_Confession" title="Augsburg Confession">Augsburg Confession</a> might be recognised as a Catholic statement of faith.<sup id="cite_ref-52" class="reference"><a href="#cite_note-52">&#91;52&#93;</a></sup><sup id="cite_ref-53" class="reference"><a href="#cite_note-53">&#91;53&#93;</a></sup> Several of Benedict's former students became his confidantes, notably <a href="/wiki/Christoph_Sch%C3%B6nborn" title="Christoph Schönborn">Christoph Schönborn</a>, and a number of his former students sometimes meet for discussions.<sup id="cite_ref-zenit1_54-0" class="reference"><a href="#cite_note-zenit1-54">&#91;54&#93;</a></sup><sup id="cite_ref-catholicherald1_55-0" class="reference"><a href="#cite_note-catholicherald1-55">&#91;55&#93;</a></sup> He served as Vice President of the University of Regensburg from 1976 to 1977.<sup id="cite_ref-56" class="reference"><a href="#cite_note-56">&#91;56&#93;</a></sup> On 26 May 1976, he was appointed a <a href="/wiki/Prelate_of_Honour_of_His_Holiness" title="Prelate of Honour of His Holiness">Prelate of Honour of His Holiness</a>.<sup id="cite_ref-57" class="reference"><a href="#cite_note-57">&#91;57&#93;</a></sup>
</p>
<h3><span id="Archbishop_of_Munich_and_Freising:_1977.E2.80.931982"></span><span class="mw-headline" id="Archbishop_of_Munich_and_Freising:_1977–1982">Archbishop of Munich and Freising: 1977&#8211;1982</span></h3>
<div class="thumb tright"><div class="thumbinner" style="width:242px;"><a href="/wiki/File:Palais_Holnstein_Munich.JPG" class="image"><img alt="" src="//upload.wikimedia.org/wikipedia/commons/thumb/c/c2/Palais_Holnstein_Munich.JPG/240px-Palais_Holnstein_Munich.JPG" decoding="async" width="240" height="180" class="thumbimage" srcset="//upload.wikimedia.org/wikipedia/commons/thumb/c/c2/Palais_Holnstein_Munich.JPG/360px-Palais_Holnstein_Munich.JPG 1.5x, //upload.wikimedia.org/wikipedia/commons/thumb/c/c2/Palais_Holnstein_Munich.JPG/480px-Palais_Holnstein_Munich.JPG 2x" data-file-width="1600" data-file-height="1200" /></a>  <div class="thumbcaption"><div class="magnify"><a href="/wiki/File:Palais_Holnstein_Munich.JPG" class="internal" title="Enlarge"></a></div><a href="/wiki/Holnstein_Palace" title="Holnstein Palace">Palais Holnstein</a> in Munich, the residence of Benedict as <a href="/wiki/Archbishop_of_Munich_and_Freising" class="mw-redirect" title="Archbishop of Munich and Freising">Archbishop of Munich and Freising</a></div></div></div>
<p>On 24 March 1977, Ratzinger was appointed <a href="/wiki/Archdiocese_of_Munich_and_Freising" class="mw-redirect" title="Archdiocese of Munich and Freising">Archbishop of Munich and Freising</a>, and was ordained a <a href="/wiki/Bishop_in_the_Catholic_Church" class="mw-redirect" title="Bishop in the Catholic Church">bishop</a> on 28 May. He took as his episcopal motto <span title="Latin-language text"><i lang="la">Cooperatores veritatis</i></span> (<a href="/wiki/Latin" title="Latin">Latin</a> for 'cooperators of the truth'),<sup id="cite_ref-58" class="reference"><a href="#cite_note-58">&#91;58&#93;</a></sup> from the <a href="/wiki/Third_Epistle_of_John" title="Third Epistle of John">Third Epistle of John</a>,<sup id="cite_ref-59" class="reference"><a href="#cite_note-59">&#91;59&#93;</a></sup> a choice on which he commented in his autobiographical work <i>Milestones</i>.<sup id="cite_ref-60" class="reference"><a href="#cite_note-60">&#91;60&#93;</a></sup>
</p><p>In the consistory of 27 June 1977, he was named Cardinal Priest of <a href="/wiki/Santa_Maria_Consolatrice_al_Tiburtino" title="Santa Maria Consolatrice al Tiburtino">Santa Maria Consolatrice al Tiburtino</a> by <a href="/wiki/Pope_Paul_VI" title="Pope Paul VI">Pope Paul VI</a>. By the time of the <a href="/wiki/2005_papal_conclave" title="2005 papal conclave">2005 conclave</a>, he was one of only fourteen remaining cardinals appointed by Paul VI, and one of only three of those under the age of 80. Of these, only he and <a href="/wiki/William_Wakefield_Baum" title="William Wakefield Baum">William Wakefield Baum</a> took part in the conclave.<sup id="cite_ref-61" class="reference"><a href="#cite_note-61">&#91;61&#93;</a></sup>
</p>
<h3><span id="Prefect_of_the_Congregation_for_the_Doctrine_of_the_Faith:_1981.E2.80.932005"></span><span class="mw-headline" id="Prefect_of_the_Congregation_for_the_Doctrine_of_the_Faith:_1981–2005">Prefect of the Congregation for the Doctrine of the Faith: 1981–2005</span></h3>
<link rel="mw-deduplicated-inline-style" href="mw-data:TemplateStyles:r1033289096"/><div role="note" class="hatnote navigation-not-searchable">Main article: <a href="/wiki/Joseph_Ratzinger_as_Prefect_of_the_Congregation_for_the_Doctrine_of_the_Faith" title="Joseph Ratzinger as Prefect of the Congregation for the Doctrine of the Faith">Joseph Ratzinger as Prefect of the Congregation for the Doctrine of the Faith</a></div>
<p>On 25 November 1981, Pope John Paul II, upon the retirement of <a href="/wiki/Franjo_%C5%A0eper" title="Franjo Šeper">Franjo Šeper</a>, named Ratzinger as the Prefect of the <a href="/wiki/Sacred_Congregation_for_the_Doctrine_of_the_Faith" class="mw-redirect" title="Sacred Congregation for the Doctrine of the Faith">Sacred Congregation for the Doctrine of the Faith</a>, formerly known as the "Sacred Congregation of the <a href="/wiki/Holy_Office" class="mw-redirect" title="Holy Office">Holy Office</a>", the historical <a href="/wiki/Roman_Inquisition" title="Roman Inquisition">Roman Inquisition</a>. Consequently, he resigned from his post in Munich in early 1982. He was promoted within the College of Cardinals to become <a href="/wiki/Bishop_of_Velletri-Segni" class="mw-redirect" title="Bishop of Velletri-Segni">Cardinal Bishop of Velletri-Segni</a> in 1993 and was made the college's vice-dean in 1998 and <a href="/wiki/Dean_of_the_College_of_Cardinals" title="Dean of the College of Cardinals">dean</a> in 2002. Just a year after its foundation in 1990, Ratzinger joined the <a href="/wiki/European_Academy_of_Sciences_and_Arts" title="European Academy of Sciences and Arts">European Academy of Sciences and Arts</a> in Salzburg.<sup id="cite_ref-62" class="reference"><a href="#cite_note-62">&#91;62&#93;</a></sup><sup id="cite_ref-63" class="reference"><a href="#cite_note-63">&#91;63&#93;</a></sup>
</p>
<div class="thumb tleft"><div class="thumbinner" style="width:172px;"><a href="/wiki/File:Ratzinger-Rom88.JPG" class="image"><img alt="" src="//upload.wikimedia.org/wikipedia/commons/thumb/8/84/Ratzinger-Rom88.JPG/170px-Ratzinger-Rom88.JPG" decoding="async" width="170" height="232" class="thumbimage" srcset="//upload.wikimedia.org/wikipedia/commons/thumb/8/84/Ratzinger-Rom88.JPG/255px-Ratzinger-Rom88.JPG 1.5x, //upload.wikimedia.org/wikipedia/commons/thumb/8/84/Ratzinger-Rom88.JPG/340px-Ratzinger-Rom88.JPG 2x" data-file-width="720" data-file-height="984" /></a>  <div class="thumbcaption"><div class="magnify"><a href="/wiki/File:Ratzinger-Rom88.JPG" class="internal" title="Enlarge"></a></div>Cardinal Ratzinger in Rome, 12 October 1988</div></div></div>
<p>Ratzinger defended and reaffirmed Catholic doctrine, including teaching on topics such as <a href="/wiki/Birth_control" title="Birth control">birth control</a>, homosexuality, and inter-religious dialogue. The theologian <a href="/wiki/Leonardo_Boff" title="Leonardo Boff">Leonardo Boff</a>, for example, was suspended, while others such as <a href="/wiki/Matthew_Fox_(priest)" title="Matthew Fox (priest)">Matthew Fox</a> were censured. Other issues also prompted condemnations or revocations of rights to teach: for instance, some posthumous writings of <a href="/wiki/Jesuit" class="mw-redirect" title="Jesuit">Jesuit</a> priest <a href="/wiki/Anthony_de_Mello" title="Anthony de Mello">Anthony de Mello</a> were the subject of a <a href="/wiki/Notification_(Holy_See)" title="Notification (Holy See)">notification</a>. Ratzinger and the congregation viewed many of them, particularly the later works, as having an element of <a href="/wiki/Religious_indifferentism" class="mw-redirect" title="Religious indifferentism">religious indifferentism</a> (in other words, that Christ was "one master alongside others"). In particular, <i><a href="/wiki/Dominus_Iesus" title="Dominus Iesus">Dominus Iesus</a></i>, published by the congregation in the jubilee year 2000, reaffirmed many recently "unpopular" ideas, including the Catholic Church's position that "salvation is found in no one else, for there is no other name under heaven given to men by which we must be saved." The document angered many Protestant churches by claiming that they are not churches, but "ecclesial communities".<sup id="cite_ref-64" class="reference"><a href="#cite_note-64">&#91;64&#93;</a></sup>
</p><p>Ratzinger's 2001 letter <i><a href="/wiki/De_delictis_gravioribus" title="De delictis gravioribus">De delictis gravioribus</a></i> clarified the confidentiality of internal church investigations, as defined in the 1962 document <i><a href="/wiki/Crimen_Sollicitationis" class="mw-redirect" title="Crimen Sollicitationis">Crimen Sollicitationis</a></i>, into accusations made against priests of certain crimes, including <a href="/wiki/Sexual_abuse" title="Sexual abuse">sexual abuse</a>. This became a subject of controversy during the <a href="/wiki/Catholic_Church_sexual_abuse_cases" title="Catholic Church sexual abuse cases">sex abuse cases</a>.<sup id="cite_ref-observer-2001-let_65-0" class="reference"><a href="#cite_note-observer-2001-let-65">&#91;65&#93;</a></sup> For 20 years, Ratzinger had been the man in charge of enforcing the document.<sup id="cite_ref-BBC2006Doc_66-0" class="reference"><a href="#cite_note-BBC2006Doc-66">&#91;66&#93;</a></sup>
</p><p>While bishops hold the secrecy pertained only internally, and did not preclude investigation by civil law enforcement, the letter was often seen as promoting a coverup.<sup id="cite_ref-67" class="reference"><a href="#cite_note-67">&#91;67&#93;</a></sup> Later, as pope, he was accused in a lawsuit of conspiring to cover up the molestation of three boys in <a href="/wiki/Texas" title="Texas">Texas</a>, but sought and obtained <a href="/wiki/Diplomatic_immunity" title="Diplomatic immunity">diplomatic immunity</a> from liability.<sup id="cite_ref-68" class="reference"><a href="#cite_note-68">&#91;68&#93;</a></sup>
</p><p>On 12 March 1983, Ratzinger, as prefect, notified the lay faithful and the clergy that Archbishop <a href="/wiki/Ng%C3%B4_%C4%90%C3%ACnh_Th%E1%BB%A5c" title="Ngô Đình Thục">Pierre Martin Ngô Đình Thục</a> had incurred <a href="/wiki/Excommunication" title="Excommunication">excommunication</a> <i><a href="/wiki/Latae_sententiae" class="mw-redirect" title="Latae sententiae">latae sententiae</a></i> for <a href="/wiki/Valid_but_illicit" class="mw-redirect" title="Valid but illicit">illicit</a> episcopal consecrations without the apostolic mandate. In 1997, when he turned 70, Ratzinger asked Pope John Paul<span class="nowrap">&#160;</span>II for permission to leave the Congregation of the Doctrine of Faith and to become an archivist in the <a href="/wiki/Vatican_Secret_Archives" class="mw-redirect" title="Vatican Secret Archives">Vatican Secret Archives</a> and a librarian in the <a href="/wiki/Vatican_Library" title="Vatican Library">Vatican Library</a>, but John Paul refused his assent.<sup id="cite_ref-69" class="reference"><a href="#cite_note-69">&#91;69&#93;</a></sup>
</p><p>Ratzinger engaged in <a href="/wiki/J%C3%BCrgen_Habermas#XVI" title="Jürgen Habermas">a dialogue with critical theorist Jürgen Habermas</a> published years later by <a href="/wiki/Ignatius_Press" title="Ignatius Press">Ignatius Press</a>.<sup id="cite_ref-70" class="reference"><a href="#cite_note-70">&#91;70&#93;</a></sup><sup class="noprint Inline-Template noprint Template-Fact" style="white-space:nowrap;">&#91;<i><a href="/wiki/Wikipedia:No_original_research#Primary,_secondary_and_tertiary_sources" title="Wikipedia:No original research"><span title="This claim needs references to reliable secondary sources. (January 2023)">non-primary source needed</span></a></i>&#93;</sup>
</p>
<h2><span id="Papacy:_2005.E2.80.932013"></span><span class="mw-headline" id="Papacy:_2005–2013">Papacy: 2005–2013</span></h2>
<div class="thumb tright"><div class="thumbinner" style="width:222px;"><a href="/wiki/File:Pope_Benedict_XVI.jpg" class="image"><img alt="" src="//upload.wikimedia.org/wikipedia/commons/thumb/3/30/Pope_Benedict_XVI.jpg/220px-Pope_Benedict_XVI.jpg" decoding="async" width="220" height="167" class="thumbimage" srcset="//upload.wikimedia.org/wikipedia/commons/thumb/3/30/Pope_Benedict_XVI.jpg/330px-Pope_Benedict_XVI.jpg 1.5x, //upload.wikimedia.org/wikipedia/commons/thumb/3/30/Pope_Benedict_XVI.jpg/440px-Pope_Benedict_XVI.jpg 2x" data-file-width="2126" data-file-height="1616" /></a>  <div class="thumbcaption"><div class="magnify"><a href="/wiki/File:Pope_Benedict_XVI.jpg" class="internal" title="Enlarge"></a></div>Benedict in <a href="/wiki/St._Peter%27s_Square" title="St. Peter&#39;s Square">St.&#160;Peter's&#160;Square</a></div></div></div>
<div class="thumb tright"><div class="thumbinner" style="width:222px;"><a href="/wiki/File:Papst_Benedikt_XVI.,_Pfingstmesse_im_Petersdom,_15._Mai_2005.jpg" class="image"><img alt="" src="//upload.wikimedia.org/wikipedia/commons/thumb/6/69/Papst_Benedikt_XVI.%2C_Pfingstmesse_im_Petersdom%2C_15._Mai_2005.jpg/220px-Papst_Benedikt_XVI.%2C_Pfingstmesse_im_Petersdom%2C_15._Mai_2005.jpg" decoding="async" width="220" height="286" class="thumbimage" srcset="//upload.wikimedia.org/wikipedia/commons/thumb/6/69/Papst_Benedikt_XVI.%2C_Pfingstmesse_im_Petersdom%2C_15._Mai_2005.jpg/330px-Papst_Benedikt_XVI.%2C_Pfingstmesse_im_Petersdom%2C_15._Mai_2005.jpg 1.5x, //upload.wikimedia.org/wikipedia/commons/thumb/6/69/Papst_Benedikt_XVI.%2C_Pfingstmesse_im_Petersdom%2C_15._Mai_2005.jpg/440px-Papst_Benedikt_XVI.%2C_Pfingstmesse_im_Petersdom%2C_15._Mai_2005.jpg 2x" data-file-width="1476" data-file-height="1917" /></a>  <div class="thumbcaption"><div class="magnify"><a href="/wiki/File:Papst_Benedikt_XVI.,_Pfingstmesse_im_Petersdom,_15._Mai_2005.jpg" class="internal" title="Enlarge"></a></div>Benedict XVI, <a href="/wiki/St._Peter%27s_Basilica" title="St. Peter&#39;s Basilica">St. Peter's Basilica</a>, 15 May 2005</div></div></div>
<div class="thumb tright"><div class="thumbinner" style="width:222px;"><a href="/wiki/File:Pope_Benedict_XVI_Delivers_Angelus.jpg" class="image"><img alt="" src="//upload.wikimedia.org/wikipedia/commons/thumb/3/3e/Pope_Benedict_XVI_Delivers_Angelus.jpg/220px-Pope_Benedict_XVI_Delivers_Angelus.jpg" decoding="async" width="220" height="147" class="thumbimage" srcset="//upload.wikimedia.org/wikipedia/commons/thumb/3/3e/Pope_Benedict_XVI_Delivers_Angelus.jpg/330px-Pope_Benedict_XVI_Delivers_Angelus.jpg 1.5x, //upload.wikimedia.org/wikipedia/commons/thumb/3/3e/Pope_Benedict_XVI_Delivers_Angelus.jpg/440px-Pope_Benedict_XVI_Delivers_Angelus.jpg 2x" data-file-width="1463" data-file-height="975" /></a>  <div class="thumbcaption"><div class="magnify"><a href="/wiki/File:Pope_Benedict_XVI_Delivers_Angelus.jpg" class="internal" title="Enlarge"></a></div>Benedict reciting the weekly Angelus prayer while overlooking Saint Peter's Square</div></div></div>
<h3><span class="mw-headline" id="Election_to_the_papacy">Election to the papacy</span></h3>
<link rel="mw-deduplicated-inline-style" href="mw-data:TemplateStyles:r1033289096"/><div role="note" class="hatnote navigation-not-searchable">Main article: <a href="/wiki/Papal_conclave,_2005" class="mw-redirect" title="Papal conclave, 2005">Papal conclave, 2005</a></div>
<p>In April 2005, before his election as pope, Ratzinger was identified as one of the <a href="/wiki/Time_100" title="Time 100">100 most influential people in the world</a> by <i><a href="/wiki/Time_(magazine)" title="Time (magazine)">Time</a></i>.<sup id="cite_ref-Time_100_71-0" class="reference"><a href="#cite_note-Time_100-71">&#91;71&#93;</a></sup> While Prefect of the Congregation for the Doctrine of the Faith, Ratzinger repeatedly stated he would like to retire to his house in the Bavarian village of <a href="/wiki/Pentling" title="Pentling">Pentling</a> near <a href="/wiki/Regensburg" title="Regensburg">Regensburg</a> and dedicate himself to writing books.<sup id="cite_ref-72" class="reference"><a href="#cite_note-72">&#91;72&#93;</a></sup>
</p><p>At the <a href="/wiki/Papal_conclave" title="Papal conclave">papal conclave</a>, "it was, if not Ratzinger, who? And as they came to know him, the question became, why not Ratzinger?"<sup id="cite_ref-goodstein_73-0" class="reference"><a href="#cite_note-goodstein-73">&#91;73&#93;</a></sup><sup id="cite_ref-74" class="reference"><a href="#cite_note-74">&#91;74&#93;</a></sup> On 19 April 2005, he was elected on the second day after four ballots.<sup id="cite_ref-goodstein_73-1" class="reference"><a href="#cite_note-goodstein-73">&#91;73&#93;</a></sup> Cardinal <a href="/wiki/Cormac_Murphy-O%27Connor" title="Cormac Murphy-O&#39;Connor">Cormac Murphy-O'Connor</a> described the final vote, "It's very solemn when you go up one by one to put your vote in the urn and you're looking up at the <a href="/wiki/Last_Judgement_(Michelangelo)" class="mw-redirect" title="Last Judgement (Michelangelo)">Last Judgement</a> of <a href="/wiki/Michelangelo" title="Michelangelo">Michelangelo</a>. And I still remember vividly the then Cardinal Ratzinger sitting on the edge of his chair."<sup id="cite_ref-75" class="reference"><a href="#cite_note-75">&#91;75&#93;</a></sup> Ratzinger had hoped to retire peacefully and said that "At a certain point, I prayed to God 'please don't do this to me'...Evidently, this time He didn't listen to me."<sup id="cite_ref-76" class="reference"><a href="#cite_note-76">&#91;76&#93;</a></sup>
</p><p>At the balcony, Benedict's first words to the crowd, given in Italian before he gave the traditional <i><a href="/wiki/Urbi_et_Orbi" title="Urbi et Orbi">Urbi et Orbi</a></i> blessing in Latin, were:
</p>
<style data-mw-deduplicate="TemplateStyles:r996844942">.mw-parser-output .templatequote{overflow:hidden;margin:1em 0;padding:0 40px}.mw-parser-output .templatequote .templatequotecite{line-height:1.5em;text-align:left;padding-left:1.6em;margin-top:0}</style><blockquote class="templatequote"><p>Dear brothers and sisters, after the great Pope John Paul II, the Cardinals have elected me, a simple, humble labourer in the vineyard of the Lord. The fact that the Lord knows how to work and to act even with insufficient instruments comforts me, and above all I entrust myself to your prayers. In the joy of the Risen Lord, confident of his unfailing help, let us move forward. The Lord will help us, and Mary, His Most Holy Mother, will be on our side. Thank you.<sup id="cite_ref-77" class="reference"><a href="#cite_note-77">&#91;77&#93;</a></sup></p></blockquote>
<p>On 24 April, Benedict celebrated the <a href="/wiki/Papal_Inauguration" class="mw-redirect" title="Papal Inauguration">Papal Inauguration</a> Mass in <a href="/wiki/St._Peter%27s_Square" title="St. Peter&#39;s Square">St. Peter's Square</a>, during which he was invested with the <a href="/wiki/Pallium" title="Pallium">Pallium</a> and the <a href="/wiki/Ring_of_the_Fisherman" title="Ring of the Fisherman">Ring of the Fisherman</a>.<sup id="cite_ref-78" class="reference"><a href="#cite_note-78">&#91;78&#93;</a></sup> On 7 May, he took possession of his cathedral church, the <a href="/wiki/Archbasilica_of_St._John_Lateran" class="mw-redirect" title="Archbasilica of St. John Lateran">Archbasilica of St. John Lateran</a>.<sup id="cite_ref-79" class="reference"><a href="#cite_note-79">&#91;79&#93;</a></sup>
</p>
<h3><span class="mw-headline" id="Choice_of_name">Choice of name</span></h3>
<p>Benedict XVI chose his <a href="/wiki/Papal_name" title="Papal name">papal name</a>, which comes from the Latin word meaning "the blessed", in honour of both <a href="/wiki/Benedict_XV" class="mw-redirect" title="Benedict XV">Benedict XV</a> and <a href="/wiki/Benedict_of_Nursia" title="Benedict of Nursia">Benedict of Nursia</a>.<sup id="cite_ref-80" class="reference"><a href="#cite_note-80">&#91;80&#93;</a></sup> Benedict XV was pope during the First World War, during which time he passionately pursued peace between the warring nations. St. Benedict of Nursia was the founder of the <a href="/wiki/Order_of_Saint_Benedict" class="mw-redirect" title="Order of Saint Benedict">Benedictine</a> monasteries (most monasteries of the Middle Ages were of the Benedictine order) and the author of the <i><a href="/wiki/Rule_of_Saint_Benedict" title="Rule of Saint Benedict">Rule of Saint Benedict</a></i>, which is still the most influential writing regarding the monastic life of <a href="/wiki/Western_Christianity" title="Western Christianity">Western Christianity</a>. The Pope explained his choice of name during his first general audience in St. Peter's Square, on 27 April 2005:
</p>
<link rel="mw-deduplicated-inline-style" href="mw-data:TemplateStyles:r996844942"/><blockquote class="templatequote"><p>Filled with sentiments of awe and thanksgiving, I wish to speak of why I chose the name Benedict. Firstly, I remember Pope Benedict XV, that courageous prophet of peace, who guided the Church through turbulent times of war. In his footsteps, I place my ministry in the service of reconciliation and harmony between peoples. Additionally, I recall Saint Benedict of Nursia, co-<a href="/wiki/Patron_saint" title="Patron saint">patron</a> of Europe, whose life evokes the Christian roots of Europe. I ask him to help us all to hold firm to the centrality of Christ in our Christian life: May Christ always take first place in our thoughts and actions!<sup id="cite_ref-81" class="reference"><a href="#cite_note-81">&#91;81&#93;</a></sup></p></blockquote>
<h3><span class="mw-headline" id="Tone_of_papacy">Tone of papacy</span></h3>
<div class="thumb tright"><div class="thumbinner" style="width:222px;"><a href="/wiki/File:Benedikt_XVI_im_Papamobil.jpg" class="image"><img alt="" src="//upload.wikimedia.org/wikipedia/commons/thumb/1/1f/Benedikt_XVI_im_Papamobil.jpg/220px-Benedikt_XVI_im_Papamobil.jpg" decoding="async" width="220" height="247" class="thumbimage" srcset="//upload.wikimedia.org/wikipedia/commons/thumb/1/1f/Benedikt_XVI_im_Papamobil.jpg/330px-Benedikt_XVI_im_Papamobil.jpg 1.5x, //upload.wikimedia.org/wikipedia/commons/thumb/1/1f/Benedikt_XVI_im_Papamobil.jpg/440px-Benedikt_XVI_im_Papamobil.jpg 2x" data-file-width="450" data-file-height="506" /></a>  <div class="thumbcaption"><div class="magnify"><a href="/wiki/File:Benedikt_XVI_im_Papamobil.jpg" class="internal" title="Enlarge"></a></div>Benedict XVI's first trip in a <a href="/wiki/Popemobile" title="Popemobile">popemobile</a></div></div></div>
<p>During Benedict's inaugural Mass, the previous custom of every cardinal submitting to the pope was replaced by being greeted by twelve people, including cardinals, clergy, religious, a married couple and their child, and newly some who were newly <a href="/wiki/Confirmation" title="Confirmation">confirmed</a> people; the cardinals had formally sworn their obedience upon the election of the new pontiff. He began using an open-topped <a href="/wiki/Popemobile" title="Popemobile">papal car</a>, saying that he wanted to be closer to the people. Benedict continued the tradition of his predecessor John Paul II and baptised several infants in the <a href="/wiki/Sistine_Chapel" title="Sistine Chapel">Sistine Chapel</a> at the beginning of each year, on the <a href="/wiki/Baptism_of_the_Lord" class="mw-redirect" title="Baptism of the Lord">Feast of the Baptism of the Lord</a>, in his pastoral role as <a href="/wiki/Bishop_of_Rome" class="mw-redirect" title="Bishop of Rome">Bishop of Rome</a>.<sup id="cite_ref-82" class="reference"><a href="#cite_note-82">&#91;82&#93;</a></sup>
</p>
<h4><span class="mw-headline" id="Beatifications">Beatifications</span></h4>
<p>On 9 May 2005, Benedict XVI began the <a href="/wiki/Beatification" title="Beatification">beatification</a> process for his predecessor, Pope John Paul<span class="nowrap">&#160;</span>II. Normally, five years must pass after a person's death before the beatification process can begin. However, in an audience with Benedict, <a href="/wiki/Camillo_Ruini" title="Camillo Ruini">Camillo Ruini</a>, vicar general of the Diocese of Rome and the official responsible for promoting the <a href="/wiki/Canonization" title="Canonization">cause for canonization</a> of any person who dies within that diocese, cited "exceptional circumstances" which suggested that the waiting period could be waived. This happened before, when Pope Paul<span class="nowrap">&#160;</span>VI waived the five-year rule and announced beatification processes for two of his predecessors, <a href="/wiki/Pope_Pius_XII" title="Pope Pius XII">Pope Pius&#160;XII</a> and <a href="/wiki/Pope_John_XXIII" title="Pope John XXIII">Pope John&#160;XXIII</a>. Benedict<span class="nowrap">&#160;</span>XVI followed this precedent when he waived the five-year rule for John Paul<span class="nowrap">&#160;</span>II.<sup id="cite_ref-83" class="reference"><a href="#cite_note-83">&#91;83&#93;</a></sup> The decision was announced on 13 May 2005, the Feast of <a href="/wiki/Our_Lady_of_F%C3%A1tima" title="Our Lady of Fátima">Our Lady of Fátima</a> and the 24th anniversary of the attempt on John Paul<span class="nowrap">&#160;</span>II's life.<sup id="cite_ref-84" class="reference"><a href="#cite_note-84">&#91;84&#93;</a></sup> John Paul&#160;II often credited Our Lady of Fátima for preserving him on that day. Cardinal Ruini inaugurated the diocesan phase of the cause for beatification in the Lateran Basilica on 28 June 2005.<sup id="cite_ref-85" class="reference"><a href="#cite_note-85">&#91;85&#93;</a></sup>
</p><p>The first beatification under the new pope was celebrated on 14 May 2005, by <a href="/wiki/Jos%C3%A9_Cardinal_Saraiva_Martins" class="mw-redirect" title="José Cardinal Saraiva Martins">José Cardinal Saraiva Martins</a>, Cardinal Prefect of the <a href="/wiki/Congregation_for_the_Causes_of_Saints" class="mw-redirect" title="Congregation for the Causes of Saints">Congregation for the Causes of Saints</a>. The new Blesseds were <a href="/wiki/Marianne_Cope" title="Marianne Cope">Marianne Cope</a> and <a href="/wiki/Ascensi%C3%B3n_Nicol_Go%C3%B1i" class="mw-redirect" title="Ascensión Nicol Goñi">Ascensión Nicol Goñi</a>. Cardinal <a href="/wiki/Clemens_August_Graf_von_Galen" title="Clemens August Graf von Galen">Clemens August Graf von Galen</a> was beatified on 9 October 2005. <a href="/wiki/Mariano_de_la_Mata" title="Mariano de la Mata">Mariano de la Mata</a> was beatified in November 2006 and <a href="/wiki/Rosa_Eluvathingal" class="mw-redirect" title="Rosa Eluvathingal">Rosa Eluvathingal</a> was beatified on 3 December of that year, and <a href="/wiki/Basil_Moreau" title="Basil Moreau">Basil Moreau</a> was beatified in September 2007.<sup id="cite_ref-86" class="reference"><a href="#cite_note-86">&#91;86&#93;</a></sup> In October 2008, the following beatifications took place: <a href="/wiki/Maria_Anna_Donati" title="Maria Anna Donati">Celestine of the Mother of God</a>, Giuseppina Nicoli, Hendrina Stenmanns, Maria Rosa Flesch, Marta Anna Wiecka, <a href="/wiki/Michael_Sopocko" class="mw-redirect" title="Michael Sopocko">Michael Sopocko</a>, <a href="/wiki/Petrus_Kibe_Kasui_and_187_Companions" class="mw-redirect" title="Petrus Kibe Kasui and 187 Companions">Petrus Kibe Kasui and 187 Companions</a>, <a href="/wiki/Candelaria_of_San_Jos%C3%A9" title="Candelaria of San José">Susana Paz-Castillo Ramírez</a>, and Maria Isbael Salvat Romero.
</p><p>On 19 September 2010, during his <a href="/wiki/Pope_Benedict_XVI%27s_visit_to_the_United_Kingdom" class="mw-redirect" title="Pope Benedict XVI&#39;s visit to the United Kingdom">visit to the United Kingdom</a>, Benedict personally proclaimed the beatification of <a href="/wiki/John_Henry_Newman" title="John Henry Newman">John Henry Newman</a>.<sup id="cite_ref-Beatification_87-0" class="reference"><a href="#cite_note-Beatification-87">&#91;87&#93;</a></sup>
</p><p>Unlike his predecessor, Benedict delegated the beatification liturgical service to a cardinal. On 29 September 2005, the Congregation for the Causes of Saints issued a communiqué announcing that henceforth beatifications would be celebrated by a representative of the pope, usually the prefect of that Congregation.<sup id="cite_ref-88" class="reference"><a href="#cite_note-88">&#91;88&#93;</a></sup>
</p>
<h4><span class="mw-headline" id="Canonizations">Canonizations</span></h4>
<div class="thumb tright"><div class="thumbinner" style="width:222px;"><a href="/wiki/File:BentoXVI-43-11052007.jpg" class="image"><img alt="" src="//upload.wikimedia.org/wikipedia/commons/thumb/5/50/BentoXVI-43-11052007.jpg/220px-BentoXVI-43-11052007.jpg" decoding="async" width="220" height="144" class="thumbimage" srcset="//upload.wikimedia.org/wikipedia/commons/thumb/5/50/BentoXVI-43-11052007.jpg/330px-BentoXVI-43-11052007.jpg 1.5x, //upload.wikimedia.org/wikipedia/commons/thumb/5/50/BentoXVI-43-11052007.jpg/440px-BentoXVI-43-11052007.jpg 2x" data-file-width="2598" data-file-height="1705" /></a>  <div class="thumbcaption"><div class="magnify"><a href="/wiki/File:BentoXVI-43-11052007.jpg" class="internal" title="Enlarge"></a></div>Benedict at the canonization of the Brazilian friar <a href="/wiki/Frei_Galv%C3%A3o" title="Frei Galvão">Frei Galvão</a></div></div></div>
<p>During his pontificate, Benedict<span class="nowrap">&#160;</span>XVI canonized 45 people.<sup id="cite_ref-89" class="reference"><a href="#cite_note-89">&#91;89&#93;</a></sup> He celebrated his first canonizations on 23 October 2005 in St. Peter's Square when he canonized <a href="/wiki/Josef_Bilczewski" class="mw-redirect" title="Josef Bilczewski">Josef Bilczewski</a>, <a href="/wiki/Alberto_Hurtado" title="Alberto Hurtado">Alberto Hurtado</a>, <a href="/wiki/Zygmunt_Gorazdowski" title="Zygmunt Gorazdowski">Zygmunt Gorazdowski</a>, <a href="/wiki/Gaetano_Catanoso" title="Gaetano Catanoso">Gaetano Catanoso</a>, and <a href="/wiki/Felice_da_Nicosia" class="mw-redirect" title="Felice da Nicosia">Felice da Nicosia</a>. The canonizations were part of a mass that marked the conclusion of the <a href="/wiki/General_Assembly_of_the_Synod_of_Bishops" class="mw-redirect" title="General Assembly of the Synod of Bishops">General Assembly of the Synod of Bishops</a> and the <a href="/wiki/Year_of_the_Eucharist" title="Year of the Eucharist">Year of the Eucharist</a>.<sup id="cite_ref-90" class="reference"><a href="#cite_note-90">&#91;90&#93;</a></sup> Benedict canonized Bishop <a href="/wiki/Rafael_Guizar_Valencia" class="mw-redirect" title="Rafael Guizar Valencia">Rafael Guizar y Valencia</a>, <a href="/wiki/Theodore_Guerin" class="mw-redirect" title="Theodore Guerin">Theodore Guerin</a>, <a href="/wiki/Filippo_Smaldone" title="Filippo Smaldone">Filippo Smaldone</a>, and <a href="/wiki/Rosa_Venerini" class="mw-redirect" title="Rosa Venerini">Rosa Venerini</a> on 15 October 2006.
</p><p>During his visit to Brazil in 2007, Benedict presided over the canonization of <a href="/wiki/Frei_Galv%C3%A3o" title="Frei Galvão">Frei Galvão</a> on 11 May, while <a href="/wiki/George_Preca" title="George Preca">George Preca</a>, founder of the Malta-based <a href="/wiki/M.U.S.E.U.M." class="mw-redirect" title="M.U.S.E.U.M.">M.U.S.E.U.M.</a>, <a href="/wiki/Szymon_of_Lipnica" title="Szymon of Lipnica">Szymon of Lipnica</a>, <a href="/wiki/Charles_of_Mount_Argus" title="Charles of Mount Argus">Charles of Mount Argus</a>, and <a href="/wiki/Marie-Eug%C3%A9nie_de_J%C3%A9sus" title="Marie-Eugénie de Jésus">Marie-Eugénie de Jésus</a> were canonized in a ceremony held at the <a href="/wiki/Apostolic_Palace" title="Apostolic Palace">Vatican</a> on 3 June 2007.<sup id="cite_ref-91" class="reference"><a href="#cite_note-91">&#91;91&#93;</a></sup> Preca is the first Maltese saint since the country's conversion to Christianity in A.D. 60 when St. Paul converted the inhabitants.<sup id="cite_ref-92" class="reference"><a href="#cite_note-92">&#91;92&#93;</a></sup> In October 2008, the following canonizations took place: <a href="/wiki/Alphonsa_of_the_Immaculate_Conception" title="Alphonsa of the Immaculate Conception">Alphonsa of the Immaculate Conception</a> of India,<sup id="cite_ref-93" class="reference"><a href="#cite_note-93">&#91;93&#93;</a></sup> <a href="/wiki/Gaetano_Errico" title="Gaetano Errico">Gaetano Errico</a>, <a href="/wiki/Narcisa_de_Jesus_Martillo_Moran" class="mw-redirect" title="Narcisa de Jesus Martillo Moran">Narcisa de Jesus Martillo Moran</a>, and <a href="/wiki/Maria_Bernarda_B%C3%BCtler" title="Maria Bernarda Bütler">Maria Bernarda Bütler</a>. In April 2009, the Pope canonized <a href="/wiki/Arcangelo_Tadini" title="Arcangelo Tadini">Arcangelo Tadini</a>, <a href="/wiki/Bernardo_Tolomei" title="Bernardo Tolomei">Bernardo Tolomei</a>, <a href="/wiki/Nuno_%C3%81lvares_Pereira" title="Nuno Álvares Pereira">Nuno Álvares Pereira</a>, <a href="/wiki/Geltrude_Comensoli" title="Geltrude Comensoli">Geltrude Comensoli</a>, and <a href="/wiki/Caterina_Volpicelli" title="Caterina Volpicelli">Caterina Volpicelli</a>.<sup id="cite_ref-94" class="reference"><a href="#cite_note-94">&#91;94&#93;</a></sup> In October of the same year he canonized <a href="/wiki/Jeanne_Jugan" title="Jeanne Jugan">Jeanne Jugan</a>, <a href="/wiki/Damien_de_Veuster" class="mw-redirect" title="Damien de Veuster">Damien de Veuster</a>, <a href="/wiki/Zygmunt_Szcz%C4%99sny_Feli%C5%84ski" title="Zygmunt Szczęsny Feliński">Zygmunt Szczęsny Feliński</a>, <a href="/wiki/Francisco_Coll_Guitart" title="Francisco Coll Guitart">Francisco Coll Guitart</a>, and <a href="/wiki/Rafael_Arn%C3%A1iz_Bar%C3%B3n" class="mw-redirect" title="Rafael Arnáiz Barón">Rafael Arnáiz Barón</a>.<sup id="cite_ref-95" class="reference"><a href="#cite_note-95">&#91;95&#93;</a></sup><sup id="cite_ref-96" class="reference"><a href="#cite_note-96">&#91;96&#93;</a></sup>
</p><p>On 17 October 2010, Benedict canonized <a href="/wiki/Andr%C3%A9_Bessette" title="André Bessette">André Bessette</a>, a French-Canadian; <a href="/wiki/Stanis%C5%82aw_So%C5%82tys" class="mw-redirect" title="Stanisław Sołtys">Stanisław Sołtys</a>, a 15th-century Polish priest; Italian nuns <a href="/wiki/Giulia_Salzano" title="Giulia Salzano">Giulia Salzano</a> and <a href="/wiki/Camilla_Battista_da_Varano" title="Camilla Battista da Varano">Camilla Battista da Varano</a>; Spanish nun <a href="/wiki/Candida_Maria_de_Jesus_Cipitria_y_Barriola" class="mw-redirect" title="Candida Maria de Jesus Cipitria y Barriola">Candida Maria de Jesus Cipitria y Barriola</a>; and the first Australian saint, <a href="/wiki/Mary_MacKillop" title="Mary MacKillop">Mary MacKillop</a>.<sup id="cite_ref-97" class="reference"><a href="#cite_note-97">&#91;97&#93;</a></sup> On 23 October 2011, he canonized three saints: a Spanish nun <a href="/wiki/Bonifacia_Rodr%C3%ADguez_y_Castro" title="Bonifacia Rodríguez y Castro">Bonifacia Rodríguez y Castro</a>, Italian archbishop <a href="/wiki/Guido_Maria_Conforti" title="Guido Maria Conforti">Guido Maria Conforti</a>, and Italian priest <a href="/wiki/Luigi_Guanella" title="Luigi Guanella">Luigi Guanella</a>.<sup id="cite_ref-98" class="reference"><a href="#cite_note-98">&#91;98&#93;</a></sup> In December 2011, the Pope formally recognized the validity of the miracles necessary to proceed with the canonizations of <a href="/wiki/Kateri_Tekakwitha" title="Kateri Tekakwitha">Kateri Tekakwitha</a>, who would be the first Native American saint; <a href="/wiki/Marianne_Cope" title="Marianne Cope">Marianne Cope</a>, a nun working with <a href="/wiki/Lepers" class="mw-redirect" title="Lepers">lepers</a> in what is now the state of Hawaii; <a href="/wiki/Giovanni_Battista_Piamarta" title="Giovanni Battista Piamarta">Giovanni Battista Piamarta</a>, an Italian priest; <a href="/wiki/Jacques_Berthieu" title="Jacques Berthieu">Jacques Berthieu</a>, a French Jesuit priest and African <a href="/wiki/Martyr" title="Martyr">martyr</a>; <a href="/wiki/Carmen_Salles_y_Barangueras" title="Carmen Salles y Barangueras">Carmen Salles y Barangueras</a>, a Spanish nun and founder of the Sisters of the Immaculate Conception; <a href="/wiki/Peter_Calungsod" class="mw-redirect" title="Peter Calungsod">Peter Calungsod</a>, a lay <a href="/wiki/Catechist" class="mw-redirect" title="Catechist">catechist</a> and martyr from the Philippines; and <a href="/wiki/Anna_Sch%C3%A4ffer" title="Anna Schäffer">Anna Schäffer</a>, whose desire to be a missionary was unfulfilled on account of her illness.<sup id="cite_ref-99" class="reference"><a href="#cite_note-99">&#91;99&#93;</a></sup> They were canonized on 21 October 2012.<sup id="cite_ref-100" class="reference"><a href="#cite_note-100">&#91;100&#93;</a></sup>
</p>
<h4><span class="mw-headline" id="Doctors_of_the_Church">Doctors of the Church</span></h4>
<p>On 7 October 2012, Benedict named <a href="/wiki/Hildegard_of_Bingen" title="Hildegard of Bingen">Hildegard of Bingen</a> and <a href="/wiki/John_of_%C3%81vila" title="John of Ávila">John of Ávila</a> as <a href="/wiki/Doctors_of_the_Church" class="mw-redirect" title="Doctors of the Church">Doctors of the Church</a>, the 34th and 35th individuals so recognized in the history of Christianity.<sup id="cite_ref-101" class="reference"><a href="#cite_note-101">&#91;101&#93;</a></sup>
</p>
<h3><span class="mw-headline" id="Curia_reform">Curia reform</span></h3>
<p>Benedict made only modest changes to the structure of the Roman Curia. In March 2006, he placed both the <a href="/wiki/Pontifical_Council_for_Pastoral_Care_of_Migrants_and_Itinerant_Peoples" class="mw-redirect" title="Pontifical Council for Pastoral Care of Migrants and Itinerant Peoples">Pontifical Council for Pastoral Care of Migrants and Itinerant Peoples</a> and the <a href="/wiki/Pontifical_Council_for_Justice_and_Peace" title="Pontifical Council for Justice and Peace">Pontifical Council for Justice and Peace</a> under a single president, Cardinal <a href="/wiki/Renato_Martino" title="Renato Martino">Renato Martino</a>. When Martino retired in 2009, each council received its own president once again. Also in March 2006, the <a href="/wiki/Pontifical_Council_for_Interreligious_Dialogue" class="mw-redirect" title="Pontifical Council for Interreligious Dialogue">Pontifical Council for Interreligious Dialogue</a> was briefly merged into the <a href="/wiki/Pontifical_Council_for_Culture" title="Pontifical Council for Culture">Pontifical Council for Culture</a> under Cardinal <a href="/wiki/Paul_Poupard" title="Paul Poupard">Paul Poupard</a>. Those Councils maintained their separate officials and staffs while their status and competencies continued unchanged, and in May 2007, Interreligious Dialogue was restored to its separate status again with its own president.<sup id="cite_ref-102" class="reference"><a href="#cite_note-102">&#91;102&#93;</a></sup> In June 2010, Benedict created the <a href="/wiki/Pontifical_Council_for_the_Promotion_of_the_New_Evangelisation" class="mw-redirect" title="Pontifical Council for the Promotion of the New Evangelisation">Pontifical Council for the Promotion of the New Evangelisation</a>, appointing Archbishop <a href="/wiki/Rino_Fisichella" title="Rino Fisichella">Rino Fisichella</a> its first president.<sup id="cite_ref-103" class="reference"><a href="#cite_note-103">&#91;103&#93;</a></sup> On 16 January 2013, the Pope transferred responsibility for <a href="/wiki/Catechesis" title="Catechesis">catechesis</a> from the <a href="/wiki/Congregation_for_the_Clergy" class="mw-redirect" title="Congregation for the Clergy">Congregation for the Clergy</a> to the Pontifical Council for Promoting the New Evangelization.<sup id="cite_ref-104" class="reference"><a href="#cite_note-104">&#91;104&#93;</a></sup>
</p>
<h3><span class="mw-headline" id="Teachings">Teachings</span></h3>
<link rel="mw-deduplicated-inline-style" href="mw-data:TemplateStyles:r1033289096"/><div role="note" class="hatnote navigation-not-searchable">See also: <a href="/wiki/Theology_of_Pope_Benedict_XVI" title="Theology of Pope Benedict XVI">Theology of Pope Benedict XVI</a></div>
<p>As pope, one of Benedict's main roles was to teach about the Catholic faith and the solutions to the problems of discerning and living the faith,<sup id="cite_ref-105" class="reference"><a href="#cite_note-105">&#91;105&#93;</a></sup> a role that he could play well as a former head of the Church's Congregation for the Doctrine of the Faith. The main points of emphasis of his teachings are stated in more detail in the Theology of Pope Benedict XVI.
</p>
<h4><span id=".22Friendship_with_Jesus_Christ.22"></span><span class="mw-headline" id="&quot;Friendship_with_Jesus_Christ&quot;">"Friendship with Jesus Christ"</span></h4>
<p>After his first <a href="/wiki/Homily" title="Homily">homily</a> as pope, Benedict referred to both Jesus Christ and John Paul II. Citing John Paul II's well-known words, "Do not be afraid! Open wide the doors for Christ!", Benedict said:
</p>
<link rel="mw-deduplicated-inline-style" href="mw-data:TemplateStyles:r996844942"/><blockquote class="templatequote"><p>Are we not perhaps all afraid in some way? If we let Christ enter fully into our lives, if we open ourselves totally to Him, are we not afraid that He might take something away from us? ... And once again the Pope said: No! If we let Christ into our lives, we lose nothing, nothing, absolutely nothing of what makes life free, beautiful, and great. No! Only in this friendship do we experience beauty and liberation. ... When we give ourselves to Him, we receive a hundredfold in return. Yes, open, open wide the doors to Christ&#160;– and you will find true life.<sup id="cite_ref-106" class="reference"><a href="#cite_note-106">&#91;106&#93;</a></sup></p></blockquote>
<p>"Friendship with Jesus Christ" was a frequent theme of Benedict's preaching.<sup id="cite_ref-107" class="reference"><a href="#cite_note-107">&#91;107&#93;</a></sup><sup id="cite_ref-108" class="reference"><a href="#cite_note-108">&#91;108&#93;</a></sup> He stressed that on this intimate friendship, "everything depends".<sup id="cite_ref-JON_109-0" class="reference"><a href="#cite_note-JON-109">&#91;109&#93;</a></sup> He also said: "We are all called to open ourselves to this friendship with God ... speaking to Him as to a friend, the only One who can make the world both good and happy ... That is all we have to do is put ourselves at His disposal ... is an extremely important message. It is a message that helps to overcome what can be considered the great temptation of our time: the claim, that after the <a href="/wiki/Big_Bang" title="Big Bang">Big Bang</a>, God withdrew from history."<sup id="cite_ref-110" class="reference"><a href="#cite_note-110">&#91;110&#93;</a></sup> Thus, in his book <i>Jesus of Nazareth</i>, his main purpose was "to help foster [in the reader] the growth of a living relationship" with Jesus Christ.<sup id="cite_ref-JON_109-1" class="reference"><a href="#cite_note-JON-109">&#91;109&#93;</a></sup> He took up this theme in his first encyclical <i><a href="/wiki/Deus_caritas_est" title="Deus caritas est">Deus caritas est</a></i>. In his explanation and summary of the encyclical, he stated: "If friendship with God becomes for us something ever more important and decisive, then we will begin to love those whom God loves and who are in need of us. God wants us to be friends of His friends and we can be so, if we are interiorly close to them."<sup id="cite_ref-111" class="reference"><a href="#cite_note-111">&#91;111&#93;</a></sup> Thus, he said that prayer is "urgently needed ... It is time to reaffirm the importance of prayer in the face of the activism and the growing secularism of many Christians engaged in charitable work."<sup id="cite_ref-112" class="reference"><a href="#cite_note-112">&#91;112&#93;</a></sup>
</p>
<h4><span id=".22Dictatorship_of_relativism.22"></span><span class="mw-headline" id="&quot;Dictatorship_of_relativism&quot;">"Dictatorship of relativism"</span></h4>
<p>Continuing what he said in the pre-conclave Mass about what he often referred to as the "central problem of our faith today",<sup id="cite_ref-113" class="reference"><a href="#cite_note-113">&#91;113&#93;</a></sup> on 6 June 2005, Benedict also said:
</p>
<link rel="mw-deduplicated-inline-style" href="mw-data:TemplateStyles:r996844942"/><blockquote class="templatequote"><p>Today, a particularly insidious obstacle to the task of education is the massive presence in our society and culture of that relativism which, recognising nothing as definitive, leaves as the ultimate criterion only the self with its desires. And under the semblance of freedom it becomes a prison for each one, for it separates people from one another, locking each person into his or her own ego.<sup id="cite_ref-114" class="reference"><a href="#cite_note-114">&#91;114&#93;</a></sup></p></blockquote>
<p>Benedict said that "a dictatorship of relativism"<sup id="cite_ref-115" class="reference"><a href="#cite_note-115">&#91;115&#93;</a></sup> was the core challenge facing the Church and humanity. At the root of this problem, he said, is <a href="/wiki/Immanuel_Kant" title="Immanuel Kant">Immanuel Kant</a>'s "self-limitation of reason". This, he said, is contradictory to the modern acclamation of science whose excellence is based on the power of reason to know the truth. He said that this self-amputation of reason leads to pathologies of religion such as terrorism and pathologies of science such as <a href="/wiki/Ecological_disaster" class="mw-redirect" title="Ecological disaster">ecological disasters</a>.<sup id="cite_ref-TT_116-0" class="reference"><a href="#cite_note-TT-116">&#91;116&#93;</a></sup> Benedict traced the failed revolutions and violent ideologies of the 20th century to a conversion of partial points of view into absolute guides. He said "Absolutizing what is not absolute but relative is called totalitarianism."<sup id="cite_ref-117" class="reference"><a href="#cite_note-117">&#91;117&#93;</a></sup>
</p>
<h4><span class="mw-headline" id="Christianity_as_religion_according_to_reason">Christianity as religion according to reason</span></h4>
<p>In the discussion with <a href="/wiki/Secularism" title="Secularism">secularism</a> and <a href="/wiki/Rationalism" title="Rationalism">rationalism</a>, one of Benedict's basic ideas can be found in his address on the "Crisis of Culture" in the West, a day before Pope John Paul II died, when he referred to Christianity as the "religion of the <i><a href="/wiki/Logos" title="Logos">Logos</a></i>" (the Greek for "word", "reason", "meaning", or "intelligence"). He said:
</p>
<link rel="mw-deduplicated-inline-style" href="mw-data:TemplateStyles:r996844942"/><blockquote class="templatequote"><p>From the beginning, Christianity has understood itself as the religion of the <i>Logos</i>, as the religion according to reason ... It has always defined men, all men without distinction, as creatures and images of God, proclaiming for them ... the same dignity. In this connection, <a href="/wiki/The_Enlightenment" class="mw-redirect" title="The Enlightenment">the Enlightenment</a> is of Christian origin and it is no accident that it was born precisely and exclusively in the realm of the Christian faith. ... It was and is the merit of the Enlightenment to have again proposed these original values of Christianity and of having given back to reason its own voice ... Today, this should be precisely [Christianity's] philosophical strength, in so far as the problem is whether the world comes from the irrational, and reason is not other than a 'sub-product,' on occasion even harmful of its development—or whether the world comes from reason, and is, as a consequence, its criterion and goal ... In the so necessary dialogue between secularists and Catholics, we Christians must be very careful to remain faithful to this fundamental line: to live a faith that comes from the <i>Logos</i>, from creative reason, and that, because of this, is also open to all that is truly rational.<sup id="cite_ref-Zenit05_118-0" class="reference"><a href="#cite_note-Zenit05-118">&#91;118&#93;</a></sup></p></blockquote>
<p>Benedict also emphasised that "Only creative reason, which in the crucified God is manifested as love, can really show us the way."<sup id="cite_ref-Zenit05_118-1" class="reference"><a href="#cite_note-Zenit05-118">&#91;118&#93;</a></sup>
</p>
<h4><span class="mw-headline" id="Encyclicals">Encyclicals</span></h4>
<p>Benedict wrote three <a href="/wiki/Encyclical" title="Encyclical">encyclicals</a>: <i><a href="/wiki/Deus_caritas_est" title="Deus caritas est">Deus caritas est</a></i> (Latin for "God is Love"), <i><a href="/wiki/Spe_Salvi" class="mw-redirect" title="Spe Salvi">Spe Salvi</a></i> ("Saved by Hope"), and <i><a href="/wiki/Caritas_in_veritate" title="Caritas in veritate">Caritas in veritate</a></i> ("Love in Truth"). In his first encyclical, <i>Deus caritas est</i>, he said that a human being, created in the image of God who is love, can practice love: to give himself to God and others (<a href="/wiki/Agape" title="Agape">agape</a>) by receiving and experiencing God's love in contemplation. This life of love, according to him, is the life of the saints such as <a href="/wiki/Mother_Teresa" title="Mother Teresa">Teresa of Calcutta</a> and the <a href="/wiki/Veneration_of_Mary_in_the_Catholic_Church" title="Veneration of Mary in the Catholic Church">Blessed Virgin Mary</a>, and is the direction Christians take when they believe that God loves them in Jesus Christ.<sup id="cite_ref-119" class="reference"><a href="#cite_note-119">&#91;119&#93;</a></sup>
</p><p>The encyclical contains almost 16,000 words in 42 paragraphs. The first half is said to have been written by Benedict in German, his first language, in the summer of 2005; the second half is derived from uncompleted writings left by his predecessor, Pope John Paul II.<sup id="cite_ref-120" class="reference"><a href="#cite_note-120">&#91;120&#93;</a></sup> The document was signed by Benedict on Christmas Day, 25 December 2005.<sup id="cite_ref-121" class="reference"><a href="#cite_note-121">&#91;121&#93;</a></sup> The encyclical was promulgated a month later in Latin and was translated into English, French, German, Italian, Polish, Portuguese, and Spanish.
